#7232. Substack job listing hints at a bigger advertising push in 2027

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Few took Substack’s co-founder and chief writing officer Hamish McKenzie literally when he told Digiday in 2025 that ads were a long way off. A new job hire suggests they should have.

The online publishing and newsletter platform is currently hiring a U.S.-based product manager of payments to help expand monetization on Substack and find better ways to support paying creators, according to the company’s careers page.

The job spec states that the scope of the role includes gifting, retention offers and improvements to the payments and subscription experience, alongside opportunities to explore commerce, and (crucially) advertising-related monetization over time. The role, which comes with a cash compensation package between $210,000 and $230,000, will also help shape future ad opportunities as the business evolves.

Substack first signalled a willingness to reconsider ads in March 2025 when Digiday spoke to McKenzie. He described ads as an “interesting business” for Substack, while suggesting it was “some way off in the distant future.”

Since making those comments, Substack has gradually started taking a more active role in connecting brands with publishers, although its efforts so far have focused on sponsorships rather than conventional ads.

Historically, ads on Substack have been creator-led rather than platform-led. Brands typically partnered directly with individual newsletters or creator networks, arranging sponsorships across newsletters and podcasts.

In June, it announced the first phase of a native sponsorship program, introducing Creator Kits to help publishers showcase their audiences and attract brand partners. In October, it introduced three sponsorship formats:

But the latest job spec suggests Substack could be considering a broader role for advertising in its monetization strategy from next year.

For some, this brings both opportunities and risks. Substack’s potential pivot toward paid advertising feels like “the end of an idealistic era,” according to Brenda Imeson, director of strategy at Brave Bison. Another platform that built its identity partly around resisting ads is now facing the commercial realities of sustaining its business.

“Subscription fatigue is biting, paywalls inevitably gatekeep good journalism and limit reach. If handled sensitively, native sponsorships will allow writers to keep more work free without hollowing out editorial integrity and could democratize access to great writing,” Imeson said. “The test will be if Substack can take brand money and keep thoughtful discourse from degrading into attention grabbing algorithmic bait.”

Marketers use creator, influencer and tastemaker interchangeably, but the industry still hasn’t settled on what the labels mean.

CEOs have never cared much for holdcos. Enterprise solutions, on the other hand, get a meeting.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

#7230. Why Qualcomm teamed with F1’s new star in race for sports marketing ROI

The company’s consumer brand Snapdragon signed 20-year-old phenom Kimi Antonelli as a brand ambassador as it looks to connect with racing’s growing audience.

Snapdragon, the consumer brand of chipmaker Qualcomm, on Tuesday launched an anthem film around its partnership with Formula 1 driver Kimi Antonelli. The 20-year-old driver for the Mercedes-AMG Petronas team — of which the brand is also a partner — became the youngest Formula 1 World Champion ever earlier this month.

The video is part of a campaign timed to the 2026 Las Vegas Grand Prix on Nov. 21. It will run on F1 broadcasts outside the U.S. on Sky, on digital and in partnership with channels including Sports Illustrated and YouTube. Apple TV is the exclusive broadcast partner of F1 in the U.S., which prevents brands like Snapdragon from running traditional broadcast ads around the race.

“We've got other ways to bring it to life,” Qualcomm CMO Don McGuire said of the campaign. “We've got creators involved, we've got remixing involved, we have slipstreaming involved… hopefully turning it into an anthem for him that has really long legs.”

A 60-second hero video follows Antonelli during a day at a hotel. Wearing smart glasses and using a tablet, both powered by Snapdragon chips, the phenom listens to a version of the traditional Neapolitan song “Funiculì, Funiculà,” trains in the gym and kicks off a social media trend. The ad ends with a tagline, “Jamme, Jamme. To the top!,” which quotes the song and speaks to his meteoric rise in racing.

“We've been watching Kimi for quite some time, and we have always believed that he was meant for greatness and that he was going to ascend very quickly. I think we made the right bet,” McGuire said.

Snapdragon’s partnership with Antonelli builds on the brand’s deal with Mercedes-AMG Petronas, a strategic collaboration that kicked off in 2023. Qualcomm had previously partnered with the team in the 2010s, a deal that was not “ROI-driven, marketing-driven,” said McGuire, who became CMO in 2021. The new partnership looks to better connect the brand’s objectives with the growing audience of F1, both in the U.S. and globally.

“The sport more and more is not something that I can judge based on [advertising value equivalency]. We have some pretty good placements on the Mercedes cars, especially with cockpit cameras and things like that, but it's just so fleeting, from a visibility standpoint,” McGuire said.

Those issues are exacerbated by the Apple TV deal, which limits brands’ ability to measure impact during broadcasts. Qualcomm is committed to F1, Mercedes-AMG Petronas and Antonelli, especially as it seeks to bring the partnership from content and storytelling through activations and hospitality.

“We believe we're already seeing higher ROI coming out of some things that we've done in the short term,” McGuire said. “We believe in the sport, why we were there and the fundamentals.”

Outside of F1, Snapdragon is no stranger to sports. Since the start of the 2024-25 season, the brand has been the principal shirt sponsor of Manchester United. Despite the iconic soccer team’s on- and off-field issues in recent years, the deal has yielded “humongous ROI” for Snapdragon, McGuire said.

“When you're dealing with a sports brand that big, at scale, the brand can withstand the ebbs and flows of performance on the pitch because the fan base is so huge,” the executive explained. “That's the benchmark that we use [for sports partnerships] and maybe it's a little unfair because they are arguably the biggest sports brand in the world.”

Snapdragon’s latest F1 marketing comes on the heels of a holiday campaign that launched earlier this month. At the heart of “The Gift That Changes Everything” is a sweet 60-second hero spot that shows how a mom and dad make Christmas seem to come sooner. The parents use a Snapdragon-powered PC to learn how to build a "time machine" that gives their child a special Christmas morning experience.

The campaign is running across broadcast, connected TV and paid social in the U.S. with CTV and paid social in the U.K., Germany and France. Created in partnership with 72andSunny New York and 72andSunny Toronto, the effort iterates on a “Snapdragon. That’s How” creative platform that launched earlier this year. The campaign has seen strong preliminary results, McGuire reported.

The holiday creative arrives as the brand prepares for a season that could see hardware makers impacted by memory shortages, reducing Snapdragon’s total addressable market at a time when the Nvidia scandal is roiling the chip market. The brand continues to work to build awareness and affinity, regardless of short-term market conditions.

“This is about building muscle and emotion that can sustain the long-term,” McGuire said. “We feel really strongly that we had to lean into holiday as if it were any other holiday season, regardless of the macro conditions.”

#7228. How brands are banking on live shopping this holiday season

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The story was first published by Digiday sibling Glossy

Many brands have accepted that, to effectively compete this holiday season, last year’s marketing playbook won’t cut it.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends. According to data from eMarketer, livestream retail sales in the U.S. are set to reach nearly $20 billion this year. And annual spend per buyer on livestreams is growing by 23% to an average of $339.27.

Brands like Tarte Cosmetics, Crocs, Beachwaver and Gap are planning to make use of live shopping, particularly on TikTok, this holiday season. Tarte and Crocs plan to launch exclusive products just for TikTok live shopping, with Crocs’ exclusives including a Disney collaboration. TikTok has seen livestream shopping sales double in the first half of this year, and use of the hashtag #holidayshopping rose 263% year over year in October.

Ziad Bon, founder of Oudware, the 3-year-old fragrance retailer that sells imported fragrances from the Middle East, told Glossy that live shopping has become an integral part of his company’s holiday strategy. Oudware already has a fairly comprehensive strategy, hosting live shopping shows on weekdays for its 215,000 followers. From Black Friday through mid-December, Oudware will be increasing its live frequency, going live on TikTok Shop every day from 2 p.m. to 7 p.m. EST. Oudware already makes more than $200,000 per month from live shopping on average and does more than $20 million in annual revenue. Its products sell for around $30-$100 per bottle.

Bon said it’s important for brands engaging in live shopping to have a plan for each live. Oudware’s live strategy is to spend the first hour of each live with what Bon called the “gift hotline,” where the team takes feedback and requests from viewers on which items and fragrances they want to see go on sale that evening. The next few hours are spent showcasing products and putting them up for sale through the regular TikTok Shop sales channel. The last hour or two of each live are reserved for auctions, a feature on TikTok Shop announced in 2025 and rolled out more broadly this year.

For Oudware, which sells about 3,000 products per day on TikTok Shop livestreams, saving the auctions for last is crucial. Live shopping conversion rates tend to be much higher than standard e-commerce, and consumers are increasingly drawn to auctions due to the possibility of lower prices. Adobe data from this year found that people are nearly 70% more likely to buy something during a live shopping stream if they see others bidding as well.

“Personally, I hate auctions,” Bon said. “A lot of our fragrance manufacturers don’t want their fragrances sold at auction, and the prices are often super low. But as a seller, it’s really hard to get engagement if you’re not doing auctions, since that’s what the customers want.”

Running auctions only at the end of the live has two purposes: It lets Oudware spend the majority of a livestream selling products at their regular prices and saves the auctions for when the live has the most viewers. More viewers means more bids, which means Oudware can sell products at auction for closer to the normal asking price.

Live shopping was a big success for brands last holiday season. TikTok Shop saw over $500 million in sales throughout the holiday period in 2025. Meanwhile, on Black Friday last year, Whatnot saw $75 million in sales in one day, tripling its previous peak from the year before.

TikTok itself is also introducing new features to support live shopping for the holidays. In the past year, big name brands and retailers like Fenty Beauty and Sephora have joined TikTok Shop. Products sold on a live will now have a visible badge if they’re part of TikTok’s Fulfilled by TikTok program (similar to Amazon Prime). Bon said the ability to ship product right after a live ends and have it arrive three days later is likely to be a big draw for customers. And, starting this fall, TikTok Shop will be working with Happy Returns to let shoppers return purchased items to any of Happy Returns’ 10,000 drop-off locations across the U.S.

“Live shopping has become one of the most engaging formats on TikTok Shop, and we expect even more brands to embrace it this holiday season as they look for new ways to connect with shoppers,” said Patrick Nommensen, president of strategic initiatives at TikTok Shop, in an email to Glossy. “What makes Live particularly compelling during the holidays is its ability to turn product discovery into an interactive shopping experience, allowing consumers to watch demonstrations, ask questions, and hear directly from brands and creators before making a purchase.”

Creators are tapping into their massive content libraries for syndication opportunities that pay dividends.

Bustle Digital Group has teamed up with Profound to help brands like Pampers and Innisfree boost their visibility in AI recommendations in a new GEO-offering.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

#7224. Maker’s Mark spotlights signature red wax top in visual refresh

The brand evokes its heritage and craftsmanship with a new color palette, website and typography.

Maker’s Mark, a Suntory Global Spirits brand, pulled back the curtain on a new global brand identity this week that updates the brand’s color palette, photography and graphic elements with a more minimal, modern aesthetic that evokes the brand’s heritage. It is live in the U.S., China, Australia and the U.K., with other markets planned throughout 2027, according to a press release.

The new visual identity spotlights the bourbon’s iconic red wax. Custom typography is inspired by the handwriting of Margie Samuels, one of the co-founders of Maker’s Mark, who died in 1985 and was responsible for creating the brand name as well as its original bottle design. The bottle design remains unchanged, per press materials. Tactile elements included in the wax seal, such as wheat stalks and oak rings, were created using materials found at Star Hill Farm, one of the properties owned by the brand and the namesake of its Star Hill Farm Whisky.

"In a world increasingly driven by speed and automation, we still believe some things are worth making slowly, by hand," said Chloe Lloyd-Jones, vice president of global brand marketing at Maker's Mark, in press materials.

The brand last made changes to its visual identity in 2021, but they were not as extensive. The latest refresh was developed in partnership with Turner Duckworth London and is part of the “Perfectly Unreasonable” campaign.

The new design will appear across digital, social and retail channels, along with a new website. The overhaul is supported by in-person experiences in the U.S. and China, along with out-of-home activations in Australia and the U.K.

The refresh comes as the bourbon industry faces significant challenges, including slowing demand and a bloated inventory, which are driving down prices. Tariffs have also taken a toll on the industry, with exports of American whiskey, which includes bourbon, falling 19% in 2025. The industry’s issues are exacerbated by the general trend of Americans drinking less overall. A little over half of Americans said they drink, according to a recent Gallup poll, the lowest it’s been since the organization started measuring the trend in 1939.

Suntory Global Spirits ceased production at its flagship Jim Beam distillery in Clermont, Kentucky at the beginning of 2026. It recently announced it would continue to pause distilling operations until at least the end of next year. Maker’s Mark is distilled at a different location, which remains operational.

“Perfectly Unreasonable” launched in 2025 and is the first campaign for the brand by Carmichael Lynch. In December, Maker’s Mark entered into its first sports league sponsorship and became the first official spirits partner of the women’s basketball league Unrivaled.

Suntory Beverage & Food Limited, which is traded on the Tokyo Stock Exchange, saw its revenue increase 9.8% year-over-year for the nine months ending Sept. 30, 2026, according to financial results.

#7223. Media Buying Briefing: Buyers grapple with incoming Amazon reporting changes

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Buying Briefing covers the latest in agency news and media buying for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Monday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Changes in the way that key media platforms measure impressions mean that performance and pricing benchmarks may be thrown into disarray come the new year.

Amazon has estimated brands will see 63% fewer impressions for display ads appearing across its retail properties, including its homepage, product pages and search results, according to a notice distributed among advertisers seen by Digiday. It’s due to the platform’s decision to shift from counting impressions the moment they begin downloading to a user’s device, to when they actually render on the user’s screen, from Jan. 1. Google announced a similar transition at the beginning of September, effective Feb. 17; spokespeople from both tech companies declined to comment.

In turn, six buyers who spoke to Digiday said they’re working to amend reporting methodologies and soothe client nerves.

Buyers fear that a reduction in reported impressions could mean that supply is constrained, driving up CPMs. “If there’s going to be fewer impressions, your CPMs will be higher,” explained Hannah Schatz, vp of programmatic operations at Coegi.

At the same time, the stricter impression reporting methodology means that lower-impact display placements further down Store pages will become even less desirable for advertisers — while top-of-page slots that stand a greater chance of influencing shoppers will see an increase in demand.

“There’s going to be probably more competition on the platform,” said Nola Ladd, awareness media supervisor at media agency Collective Measures.

Amazon has not issued guidance regarding the expected impact on pricing or costs, or whether it might update its billing methodology around the new impression reporting, according to Stagwell Media Platform’s global product managing director, Dru Sil.

“The implications are up to us to discover,” he said.

While the drop in impressions might unnerve some clients, it’s unlikely to provoke advertisers to reduce spending. Sil suggested that most advertisers will maintain spending levels, in order to establish new performance benchmarks in January and February.

“I don’t expect clients to like dramatically scale up budgets to try to get ahead of the curve. You’re likely going to continue to run what you’ve been running because you have an expected outcome that you’re trying to obtain from Amazon, regardless of the specific ad format,” said Sil.

He added: “You need to build that baseline anyway, right? And the only way to build that baseline is to be running ads.”

Benchmarking may be the real problem buyers face when they shake off the new year’s hangover.

Automated reporting processes, or media mix models that use impressions as a key input, might need to be recalibrated to account for the expected drop in reported impressions. “If you have a lot of automated processes, optimizations, reconciliations, built around these data sets already, you’re going to have to do a little bit of work,” said Paul DeJarnatt, vice president and managing director of integrated client solutions at Novus.

For most buyers, Amazon’s and Google’s tinkering with impression reporting merely underlines the importance of focusing on outcomes, rather than media KPIs, to understand campaign performance.

“The bottom line is, we don’t expect this to change campaign results. The shift will make reporting metrics look different, but it shouldn’t move ROAS or CPA, which are the outcomes our clients care about,” said Jared Smith, senior director of strategy and innovation, January Digital.

“Impressions, CPMs, they shouldn’t be north star metrics,” said Ernie DeColte, svp of media at indie agency Level. “They’re tool metrics we use to optimize towards business impact. This might be a wake-up call to a few advertisers. Maybe it’s time to think about buying differently.”

Still, communicating this change to clients who might be alarmed when they see impression counts fall off a cliff (or CPMs spike) is something media buyers across the industry are attempting to get ahead of.

“The client, even though that metric is misleading, is used to seeing a big number,” said Ladd.

“They’re used to seeing really low CPMs. They’re used to seeing really great impression volume, and that change and that difference is going to require agencies and media experts to really think about the way that they present success to the client,” she added.

Just because it’s got a donut graph doesn’t mean a report is saying very much. According to an Experian survey of 400 marketers, the profusion of dashboards and data isn’t helping marketers back up their decisions.

“In the past, a company like Unilever, buying big media in Brazil from TV Globo, had a significant competitive advantage. Now you are buying from Meta or from Google or from Amazon or from Walmart or from TikTok, and that model doesn’t favor a scale of purchase. It favors the quality of engagement you generate on the platform.”

—Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, speaking during its Capital Markets Day last week, on why better creative could be the secret to making its marketing operation work harder.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

#7222. What U.S. and international classrooms can teach us about improving math instruction

Last year, one of my strongest students could solve complex equations flawlessly--but paused when I asked a simple question: “Why does this method work?”

→ Baca artikel asli

#7219. What it will take for CMOs to reach the next frontier of AI-driven growth

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

CMOs at the largest enterprise brands across consumer goods, financial services, B2B and more are all struggling with the same thing: more AI technology has not translated into more business growth. They’re stuck chasing efficiency gains, funding tools that optimize tasks inside functional silos, running pilots that go nowhere and delegating AI decisions to IT teams.

But a few CMOs are doing it differently. Eric Schwartz, CMO of The Clorox Company, told me his marketers are building AI systems that produce highly optimized product content. They’re encoding brand voice, compliance guardrails and seasonal context into agents that work across every brand in their portfolio. Their goal isn’t to save time on doing what human writers can already do. Instead, they want to codify, scale and orchestrate the expertise that increases relevance, engagement and conversion with consumers. That’s a growth play.

This is an operating-model decision that the CMO has to own: give marketers the strategic, system-building work and shift execution to AI agents. That takes much more than a chatbot built for a solo work experience.

“I tell everyone that tries to pitch me about AI capabilities that the last thing I need is a 14th chatbot to save our marketers time,” Schwartz said. “What I need is interoperable functionality.”

Schwartz isn’t alone. A vanguard of marketers are charting the frontier of AI-native marketing. To do this requires three fundamental shifts.

Employees are becoming more efficient with AI, but report that leaders aren’t asking them to reinvest the productivity gains into strategic work; they’re just expected to produce more output. Boston Consulting Group found that 66% of frontline employees receive limited or no guidance on using saved time, and more than half don’t reinvest it into strategic work.

Measuring AI success solely on efficiency invites efficiency’s reward: a smaller budget.

For example, one CMO’s AI-powered content system is saving the company 500 hours a month. But the company’s CFO saw the dashboard and asked: “If marketing can do this with 500 fewer hours, why does the budget stay the same?” The dashboard was supposed to prove value. Instead, it made the case for a cut.

A marketing team at a regulated financial services company chose a different approach. The team was losing 360 hours a week to legal review bottlenecks, manual multi-channel content creation and constant product updates across 800-plus references. The team built a system with compliance encoded into the workflow and distribution automated across 25 channels. The result: 360 hours freed up weekly, 90% less editing time and zero agency spend.

The marketing team reinvested those hours into producing an award-winning ad campaign, not producing more of the same content. In a category where most consumers can’t name three financial lenders, breaking through the sameness drove brand recall, pipeline and market share. The system handled the production, but the humans did the work that won.

The AI opportunity isn’t time saved. Frontier CMOs reinvest that time in architecture, curation and orchestration: designing agentic workflows, encoding brand voice and steering the system to drive growth. The humans who know best what makes their brand different turn saved time into a real advantage.

Historically, marketing and revenue have operated on separate tracks. In B2B, marketing fills the top of the funnel and hands off to sales. In consumer brands, marketing creates content while commerce manages the shelf. In financial services, brand marketing and the advisors who own the relationship rarely connect. In most organizations, no single team owns the moments that decide the sale.

Sixty-seven percent of B2B buyers now prefer a rep-free experience, according to data from Gartner. Consumers and bank customers are doing their own research too. In financial services, buyers are asking AI tools for recommendations before they ever talk to an advisor. These buyers aren’t waiting for a handoff.At the frontier, there are no handoffs. AI agents carry customer data, market signals and brand and compliance context between marketing and revenue teams in real time. Context doesn’t die at a cross-functional boundary. It compounds.

Schwartz described what this looks like at Clorox. The company’s media ecosystem spans retail media, e-commerce and traditional retail, and the goal is to connect performance back to sales — or to the signals that point to sales. Most marketing organizations stop at creating and deploying campaigns. Schwartz wants that signal flowing back to show where to lean in and scale and to shape what gets created next. By closing that loop, a media budget compounds into growth instead of disappearing.

A CMO recently told me his organization has 40 active pilots, all marked green. Everything worked in the lab, but nothing survived contact with the organization. IT loved the tools, but marketing couldn’t use them.

This happens when AI adoption is delegated to IT. The CMO sponsors the budget but doesn’t own the transformation. In Writer’s 2026 survey, more than half of CMOs (52.3%) reported “significant knowledge gaps” about AI. Gartner predicts that by 2027, lack of AI literacy will rank among the top three reasons CMOs are replaced.

At the frontier, the CMO owns the entire marketing AI agenda: selecting tools, redesigning workflows and deciding what should remain human-led. That means making AI adoption a cultural shift, not a software rollout. And that can’t be delegated.

Schwartz is an example of what this looks like in practice. He made AI adoption his own rather than delegating it to an innovation team or task force.

The CMOs making progress are changing how the work gets done, moving from traditional marketing operations toward a new model of driving growth with AI. They aren’t simply doing the same work with AI layered on top, but reimagining foundational structures, systems and strategies for a new era of marketing. The frontier isn’t a place a CMO arrives at. It’s the way a CMO operates.Partner insights from Writer

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

#7216. Anatomy of an influencer marketing deal

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Ever wonder how an influencer marketing deal gets negotiated? Well, you’re in luck.

In a live Digiday Podcast recording during the recent Creator Strategies virtual event, YSL Beauty’s Hadley Musinsky and Night’s Sophie Bressler broke down how deals between brands and influencers come together. Step one usually starts with the brand.

“Whenever we have a launch or a business priority, we’ll talk through where do we need support, who do we want support from and what does that really look like. Then, as the influencer lead, we’ll talk through or present ideas, concepts, creator names that I think would be such a perfect fit for the product. And I will then reach out to someone like Sophie, who I’ve had a great relationship with for over 10 years now,” said Musinsky, director of influence and advocacy at YSL Beauty.

Then the ball is in the creator’s court, or at least the creator’s talent manager, such as Bressler. Brief in hand, she looks for the brand’s objective to identify the creators best suited to the campaign.

“If Hadley says to me, ‘We’re really looking to drive sales’, I don’t want to put forward a client who doesn’t have a history of strong link clicks on Instagram Stories,” Bressler said.

From there, the two sides go back and forth to hash out details, including exclusivity, usage rights and eventually payment terms.

“The payment terms don’t get ironed out until we get to the contracting stage,” said Bressler, who delved into details of those discussions in the episode, along with Musinsky who shared YSL Beauty and parent company’s L’Oréal’s payment terms.

Here are a few highlights from the conversation, which have been edited for length and clarity.

Musinsky: “We usually don’t include links, so we’re not tracking to that extent. Of course, we want to see a spike in sales. But there’s never this goal [of an] exact percentage-increase number. For us, it’s usually more of an awareness play, or as we call it, a virality moment of starting a conversation, having that video or multiple videos perform really well and then seeing other creators feed off of it. Usually that’s our goal.”

Bressler: “I may come to the table and say, ‘I’ve reviewed the scope of work and the creative. Based on that, this is the general starting rate for the [creator] that you’re interested in’. From there it becomes more of a conversation and a friendly negotiation. If what I’ve presented to Hadley is too high for this specific campaign, we would then work backwards to maybe scale back the scope of work, pare back the usage, remove the exclusivity, tweak the deliverables.”

Syndication and usage rights from the brand side

Musinsky: “For the most part, we go in with the offer asking for everything because we know we can start scaling back. If we’re looking for an Instagram post or TikTok, we’ll ask for syndication so that it’s posted across both platforms or even three platforms that will include YouTube Shorts. And then in terms of usage, we’ll usually ask for two weeks paid upfront. We do love to boost content. I think it’s also beneficial for the creator. That’s us putting spend behind it and it running through their account as an ad.”

Exclusivity and usage rights from the creator side

Bressler: “When it comes to paid usage, what is the perceived exclusivity there? Even if there is not technical exclusivity and my client could work with [a separate] luxe beauty brand the next day, if YSL Beauty is running paid ads with my client in it, another luxe beauty brand may not want to collaborate with [the creator] because they think they must be off the table because they’re working with YSL Beauty. We as reps need to understand what other opportunities are on the table at that specific time and weigh the opportunity cost against the desired scope of work in full.”

#7211. Dentsu makes entertainment and sport a C-suite priority amid earnings stumble

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Expertise in entertainment could be the next agency C-suite superpower. Takeshi Sano, the president and global CEO of Japanese agency holding company Dentsu, clearly thinks so: he’s elevated Mitsuyuki ‘Mike’ Nakamura, a former media buyer, TV producer and a 36-year Dentsu veteran, to serve as the group’s first global practice president for sports and entertainment.

Nakamura’s appointment is further proof of the growing importance of entertainment expertise to major ad agencies,  as media spend concentrates around live sports and TV, film and showbiz reknit around the creator economy.

“Sports and entertainment is where culture is made today, and Dentsu has a long track-record of helping brands go further than just buying space around it,” said Sano in a statement.

Nakamura has worked for Dentsu in both Thailand, as CEO of Dentsu Media, and the U.K., where he led its CX business, Dentsu X. Most recently he served as president of sports and entertainment at Dentsu Japan.

Dentsu is more closely coiled with the entertainment sector than most advertising groups. It’s an investor in the upcoming Angry Birds film, which will also feature two of its clients. It has produced microdramas for P&G and recently inked a partnership with Iconic Arts to help brands create their own anime. It’s not alone in moving closer to the showbiz world, though; last week, Ogilvy launched a partnership with top talent agency CAA to use AI clones of its high profile stars in brand advertising.

The company made the announcement the same day that it released its third-quarter earnings report. Overall organic revenue fell 3.7% and the company altered its revenue guidance for the whole of 2026 from 0.1% growth to -1%.

Organic growth reversed in Japan, the Americas (down 6.5%) and Europe, the Middle East and Africa (EMEA), where income fell 7.1% due to “macro uncertainty and reduced business from major clients” hitting media revenues. Dentsu’s media revenues in the Americas have fallen 2.5% this year so far.

The company’s results come amid signs of a potential slowdown in overall global advertising spending. Amazon, Google and Reddit each recorded deceleration in advertising revenues this past quarter; the former saw Amazon ad revenue growth fall from 26% in Q2, to 12% in Q3.

But Dentsu has fared worse than its major agency group rivals. WPP’s revenue for the same period was up 0.5%, while Publicis’ rose 5%.

And Brian Wieser, founder of Madison & Wall, suggested its revenue declines reflect its own circumstances, rather than a broader slowdown – namely the absence of Microsoft from its balance sheet. The company lost the tech giant’s $2.67 billion global account to Publicis back in April.

“Its scale should not be underestimated,” Wieser said. “It was probably by far the biggest client that Dentsu had.”

Spending on ad agencies is increasingly divorced from spending on advertising as a whole, Wieser added. “Marketers have been so focused on containing their non-working spending, as they characterize it, that the growth of spend on [advertising] is independent of the growth of spend on agencies,” he said.

The bottom line is that Dentsu can’t rely on the ad spend tides to raise its boat, but must find its own way back to growth.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

#7208. Ad Tech Briefing: Google’s antitrust troubles aren’t over

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Developments last week showed that Google faces another potential antitrust setback, this time in Europe, as it prepares for the next stage of its U.S. ad tech monopoly case.

On Nov. 12, an adviser to the European Union’s highest court recommended overturning a 2024 judgment that annulled an earlier €1.49 billion ($1.7 billion) fine against Google over its AdSense for Search business.

In her ⁠opinion, Advocate General Juliane Kokott argued that the EU’s General Court made legal errors when it overturned the European Commission’s original 2019 penalty. Specifically, Kokott concluded that the lower court had imposed excessive requirements on regulators when assessing whether Google’s contractual restrictions harmed competition.

The case concerns agreements Google used with publishers that displayed search advertisements through its AdSense for Search service; i.e., it involves a different business unit than the one under scrutiny in its most recent U.S. trial.

Per the European proceedings’ plaintiffs, between 2006 and 2016, Google imposed contractual restrictions that limited those websites’ ability to display advertisements from rival search advertising providers.

The European Commission identified three types of restrictions: exclusivity clauses preventing publishers from using competing services, placement clauses restricting where rival advertisements could appear, and provisions requiring Google’s prior approval before changes to advertising displays.

The Commission concluded that these practices constituted a single, continuous infringement of European competition law. Google challenged the decision, and in September 2024, the General Court annulled the fine, finding that regulators had failed to examine relevant circumstances adequately, particularly the duration of Google’s contractual arrangements.

Kokott’s opinion, published Nov. 12, argued that the General Court had placed excessive emphasis on the duration of individual agreements rather than considering their collective effect on competition.

In the accompanying press release, the court explained that regulators could assess contracts collectively to determine whether they could produce exclusionary effects. Kokott recommended setting aside the 2024 judgment and returning the case to the General Court for reconsideration.

Importantly, the recommendation does not automatically reinstate the original fine. The opinion is nonbinding, and the Court of Justice has yet to issue its judgment. Moreover, Kokott acknowledged that the penalty should ultimately be reduced because certain findings concerning Google’s conduct in 2016 are no longer being contested.

Nevertheless, the opinion creates the prospect of another prolonged legal battle over Google’s advertising practices, as the development comes two months after Google avoided a forced breakup of its advertising technology business in the U.S.

In September, federal judge Leonie Brinkema rejected the Justice Department’s proposed divestiture of Google’s AdX exchange and publisher ad server, DoubleClick for Publishers.

Instead, the court accepted most of the government’s proposed behavioral remedies, subject to modifications. The decision shifted the focus from Google’s ownership of its advertising infrastructure to the restrictions governing how that infrastructure operates.

The remedies are intended to address Google’s ability to favor its own advertising products, including through changes affecting interoperability, auction transparency and access for competing ad tech companies. However, the precise obligations and their enforcement remain central questions as the parties work through the final judgment.

Google has indicated that it intends to appeal the underlying liability decision, potentially extending the litigation beyond implementation of the behavioral remedies.

The European and American cases concern different markets and legal questions, but both illustrate the difficulty regulators face in challenging Google’s advertising dominance.

In Europe, the courts are still examining conduct that ended a decade ago. In the U.S., the argument has moved from whether Google violated competition law to whether the remedies will materially change market behavior.

However, even successful antitrust enforcement does not guarantee that publishers and advertisers will move their business away from Google.

Meanwhile, last week’s proceedings demonstrated how The Trade Desk’s struggles highlight a different problem — a development that embodies the scale of the challenges independent competitors to Google, etc., face when challenging Big Tech.

On Nov. 9, The Trade Desk reported a rare quarterly revenue decline, with third-quarter sales falling approximately 0.5% to $736 million from $739 million a year earlier. While the company did beat Wall Street’s (modest) expectations, the result marked a sharp reversal for a company that had long delivered double-digit growth.

The results underscored the difficulties facing the company during what executives have described as a transitional year. The slowdown comes amid growing competition from Amazon, shifting agency relationships and the increasing use of AI to automate media buying.

While The Trade Desk has invested heavily in its Kokai platform and related AI capabilities, those initiatives have yet to restore its historical growth rates, with executives there also revealing how they are reconsidering how acquisitions might contribute to its next phase of growth.

On the company’s earnings call, Jeff Green, The Trade Desk, CEO, revealed leadership were taking a closer look at potential acquisitions, suggesting a more active approach to M&A than the company has historically pursued.

For years, The Trade Desk positioned itself as an independent alternative to the advertising ecosystems operated by Google, Amazon, and other major technology companies, with this pitch a key selling point when articulating its value in the responsible use of CMOs’ media investments.

However, its latest results demonstrate that independence alone is not enough to guarantee growth.

Regulators may eventually make Google’s advertising infrastructure more accessible to competitors. But those competitors must still persuade advertisers that their platforms offer better technology, performance, and economics. The Trade Desk’s difficulties suggest that the industry’s competitive challenges extend well beyond Google’s market power.

#7204. What exactly counts as a creator? The industry still can’t agree

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Every brand seems to have different definitions when it comes to the creator economy. What one brand calls a tastemaker, another labels an influencer. If marketers can’t agree on creator taxonomy, standardization — on everything from measurement to cost — may remain further away than the industry would like.

Since the ballooning of the creator economy, marketers have been trying to standardize everything from what counts as a view, brand-creator contractual language and the framework for deal fee structures. According to the IAB’s latest creator economy report, at least 23% of marketers say the industry would benefit from a unified creator taxonomy.

“That’s the blurry line. Who’s a mega influencer and who’s a celebrity? I don’t know anymore,” said Mindy Murray, director of influencer and content at General Mills.

General Mills’ approach to creator definitions is nuanced. The highest tier is celebrity status, where partnerships often go beyond social and into product design and formula. Mega creators are positioned just below celebrity partnerships. Mid creators range from 500,000 to 1.5 million social media followers. There are super fans or brand advocates who are dedicated creators who are positing consistently on behalf of the brand. Finally, there’s friends and family, which is General Mills’ organic influencer group who also act as brand advocates.

Shutterfly has a three-tier creator codification. Tastemakers are social media stars with one million or more followers. They’re handpicked for major campaigns. Content creators fall on the other end of the spectrum, having anywhere from a couple hundred to more than 2,000 social media followers. Influencers fall somewhere in between, Callie Worthington, vp of brand marketing and creative at Shutterfly, told Digiday.

Shutterfly doesn’t have an internal pay scale that relates to its codification system. It is expected, however, that bigger creators (which could be tastemakers and influencers) command bigger budgets.

Rothy’s shoe brand also uses the term tastemakers. However, Rothy’s creator classifications are defined less by follower count and more by their cultural relevance, said Anna Doré, vp of brand and communications at Rothy’s. A tastemaker could be a TikTok star or a Substack writer. In comparison, Rothy’s refers to affiliate creators as “the lower funnel crater and conversion types,” Doré said.

Meanwhile, agencies don’t seem to go by the same guidelines. For example, VML organizes creators by their role in a campaign — salespeople, brand ambassadors, product collaborators, etc. The taxonomy isn’t as strict, Noah Eisemann, global executive director of creator and influencer at VML, told Digiday.

“Every influencer is a creator, but not every creator is an influencer,” Eisemann said.

The creator economy has exploded to where creators aren’t just selling products on behalf of brands. They’ve been hired as actors in Super Bowl spots. They’ve been brought on as product collaborators. Some are acting as creative agencies helping brands ideate and execute creative campaigns. Kim Kardashian is as much a content creator as she is an influencer, depending on what the brand asks of her, he added.

“The more useful standard may be a shared way of defining the role a creator is playing in a particular strategy — because that’s ultimately what should determine how we value the partnership and what success looks like,” Eisemann said.

A brand’s tiering setup influences how creators are evaluated, how their impact is measured and how they’re utilized in campaigns.

“When expectations, budgets, and measurement rely on different assumptions, effective work can be undervalued, and investment becomes harder to justify or sustain,” said Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP creator marketing agency.

But across the industry, there’s no conventional naming system. (Even Digiday staffers couldn’t agree on what defines a creator versus influencer.) And as more money pours into the creator economy, marketers are expecting measurement and business outcomes.

“Greater clarity around inputs and expected outcomes would make it easier to set goals, allocate budgets, and define the role creators play within the broader advertising landscape,” Stamatiou said.

A new product manager role will explore ad-related monetization, suggesting the platform could make ads a bigger part of its business.

#7201. KERV.ai, Macmillan Publishers and Chomps are among this year’s Work/Life Awards finalists

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This year’s Work/Life Awards finalists reflect a landscape where companies are rethinking workplace culture as a driver of business performance, employee engagement and innovation. Across industries, organizations are giving employees a more active role in shaping workplace policies and priorities, building recognition and connection into the everyday employee experience and extending their missions through community involvement and social impact.

KERV.ai earned a finalist spot in the Most Innovative Culture category for making employee input and cross-functional collaboration central to its workplace culture. Its quarterly HR listening tours, culture committee and real-time feedback programs gave employees opportunities to influence company policies and engagement initiatives. The emphasis on employee voice also extended to the business. Employees across product, engineering, client services and creative identified an opportunity to improve contextual targeting, leading to the development of Moment Match Engine, a video technology solution built through cross-departmental collaboration.

Macmillan Publishers is nominated in the Most Committed to Social Good category for connecting its workplace culture to a broader mission of expanding access to books, literacy and intellectual freedom. Through its MacImpact corporate social responsibility program, the publisher supports employee volunteering, charitable giving and community partnerships. In 2025, employees took part in 27 volunteer events and contributed 494 volunteer hours, a 60% increase from the previous year. Its employee-led Yours to Read Committee also advocates for access to diverse literature and the freedom to read.

Chomps, a finalist for Most Committed to Employee Appreciation, focused on maintaining employee connection as its remote-first workforce grew to more than 200 people across 30-plus states. The company brings employees together through annual retreats, departmental offsites and company-wide gatherings, while recognition programs such as Chompian of the Quarter and Chompian of the Year celebrate employee contributions. Investments in wellness, mental health days and professional development further extend appreciation beyond formal recognition.

Together, this year’s nominees represent a workplace landscape in which culture is shaped by employee participation, meaningful recognition and a commitment to a broader purpose beyond business goals. As expectations of employers continue to evolve, their approaches offer examples of how organizations can build more connected, engaged and purpose-driven workplaces.

#7196. Some creators are turning YouTube back catalogs into sizable paychecks

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Creators are turning their back catalogs into fresh revenue as platforms like Tubi hunt for more content to meet audience demand.

One true-crime creator, who requested anonymity, earned $154,000 across Spotify, Tubi, and Facebook this past September — more than 1.8 times what their channel made in the YouTube Partner Program that month, according to Chanel Simmons, md of digital media partner Motion Society, who did not provide specific YouTube figures.

That kind of return is part of a broader push to make existing content work harder. Motion Society has been syndicating creator content since 2022, but more creators and platforms are approaching them to help syndicate content now than ever. It works with over 650 creators and IP owners to distribute more than 30,000 videos a month across over 20 different digital platforms and TVs.

For Reign Maker Group CEO and co-founder Jonathan Chanti, the opportunity reflects a shift in creator strategy. “The focus is shifting from audience growth to maximizing the value of the content creators already own. Creators have spent the last decade learning how to produce content at scale. The next challenge is learning how to distribute and monetize it at scale,” he said. “Syndication is the natural evolution.”

Syndication is becoming increasingly popular in the creator space and Motion Society isn’t the only company investing in it. Lauren Lyster, vp and head of social media at Go Fish Digital, noted that agencies and managers can help individual creators syndicate. She pointed out that companies like Jellysmack have worked with creators like MrBeast and Brad Mondo, and Mirage Digital’s focus is on helping creators turn their catalogs into episodic content.

“Syndication is kind of like the new MCN [multi-channel network], but rebuilt around distribution instead of YouTube ad sales. The big bonus is that the destinations now pay. So the real value from the syndicators is operational,” said Keith Bendes, chief strategy officer at Linqia.

Kudzi Chikumbu, vp of creator partnerships at Tubi, said its audiences are always looking for authentic creator content. “This is why we look for creators whose body of work has a compelling, distinctive voice and aligns with Tubi’s fandoms, such as horror, YA, and reality,” he said. Tubi licenses content from creators like Joey Graceffa, who makes cinematic murder mystery episodes, and Kinigra Deon, who produces scripted drama and comedy series. Chikumbu said Tubi looks to partner with creators who have built something people are already invested in, which offers monetization in the form of revenue share.

“These platforms need a steady flow of programming, and creator content can help fill that need while bringing a built-in fan base with it,” said Lyster.

For creators, syndication can open additional revenue streams, but adapting and placing a catalog across platforms takes time and operational work. Companies like Motion Society handle that process, typically through revenue-share deals.

It starts its creator syndication process with curation, selecting and sequencing videos from catalogs into cohesive seasons. Then, the company re-edits the content to TV standards, which involves cutting out sponsor reads and social calls to action, removing watermarks, and editing out anything that doesn’t meet TV brand safety guidelines. The company will also cut out YouTube-specific content like mentions of previous videos or calls to follow or burned-in captions. Motion Society designers then make original key art for each show and season in platform-specific formats, and even A/B test strategies across platforms. All of this work saves creators the time and effort of adapting their catalogs for each platform. Motion Society typically takes a revenue share though it declined to disclose details.

These kinds of syndication partnerships give creators another way to monetize videos they’ve already made. As platforms seek more programming, distributors are betting there’s room to place more creator catalogs.

“Free streaming TV services are especially well positioned [to grab creators’ libraries]. Tubi has been openly building programs for creators, and services like Roku, Samsung TV Plus and Pluto TV also carry a lot of digital-first content. These platforms need a steady flow of programming, and creator content can help fill that need while bringing a built-in fan base with it,” said Lyster. She found Spotify an “interesting fit” for creators who do podcast-style video, and thinks Facebook works best for long-form content.

Syndication at this scale could result in shifting terms for brand partnerships. “Who owns the extra reach?” asked Bendes. “If brands are sponsoring the creator’s content and it gets syndicated, does that sponsorship carry? Usage rights, exclusivity windows and brand-safety clauses will need to catch up.”

Bendes expects sponsorship pricing for syndicated creators to move towards TV-style negotiation, with “a premium for multi-surface reach, and a lot of pushback from brands asking for proof that the reach is real.”

Lyster wondered if creators will start to fold syndication into how they run their business and where they post content and potentially follow a model more similar to how musicians use a distributor to get their music onto every streaming service.

Both Chanti and Lyster also noted that creator control could become increasingly more crucial if syndication takes off.

“The biggest risks are creators sacrificing control over their IP, audience relationships or future monetization opportunities for relatively modest short-term gains. Ownership, transparency and favorable licensing terms are critical,” Chanti said.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

Bustle Digital Group has teamed up with Profound to help brands like Pampers and Innisfree boost their visibility in AI recommendations in a new GEO-offering.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

#7194. Future of TV Briefing: Everyone wants a clearer CTV bidstream, but who’s required to provide it?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the latest call-to-action for the CTV advertising industry to tackle its transparency problem.

You may have heard that connected TV advertising has a transparency problem. So what’s anyone gonna do about it?

Well, the Coalition for Innovative Media Measurement has some ideas. Having surveyed 50 programmatic marketers about CTV’s signals situation, the industry organization has come up with a couple of recommendations.

First, companies need to agree to a minimum viable set of trading signals. The study’s proposed list of baseline signals includes standard parameters like IP address (as questionable a signal as it is), video placement and supply chain object (or “schain”).

These are already pretty standard signals. According to FreeWheel, one of the CIMM study’s sponsors, 95% to 100% of U.S. CTV and set-top box bid requests through its platform in February 2026 contained IP address, video placement and schain. However, other proposed baseline signals were slightly less common. For example, auction type was missing from 5% to 20% of FreeWheel’s sampled bid requests but would be table stakes under CIMM’s proposed signal set.

This gets at the underlying issue. Ad tech intermediaries don’t always support the signals that buyers and sellers want to be passing back and forth. Per the CIMM study, only 8% of the surveyed programmatic marketers — 4 out of 50 — said the demand-side platforms they use “always” are able to handle the buyers’ required signals.

To be fair, 58% of buyers said their required signals are “often” able to be handled by DSPs. But “often” is not “always,” which is the problem.

“The biggest asks that I got were from buyers and sellers that we want to understand which signals are making it all the way through the path and are actually operationalized versus used for reporting or just unusable,” said Emily Palmer, an industry consultant and chair of CIMM’s programmatic working group, who was one of the study’s authors.

Agreeing to a set of baseline signals would be one way to address this, though that would mean companies actually adhering to that agreement.

FreeWheel already requires certain signals like IP address in order to validate traffic, said Alex Strickland, head of programmatic supply relationships at the Comcast-owned ad tech provider that manages demand-side and supply-side platforms. But asked if FreeWheel requires all of the signals listed in the proposed trading baseline set, he said “we don’t require them.”

OpenX, the study’s other sponsor, takes signals into account when selecting which publishers’ bid requests to send to which DSPs, said Danner Close, vp of operations at OpenX. “We constantly engage publishers with, ‘Hey, here’s what is missing from a high-fidelity standpoint that’s preventing your request from going further or from garnering greater bid density’,” he said.

Supporting the table-stakes signals is one thing. Knowing which signals — beyond the baseline requirements (or would-be requirements) — an ad tech firm supports is another. This is where the CIMM study’s other recommendation is meant to come in. Ad tech firms would be asked to publish capability statements declaring which signals they support.

“Buyers and sellers would love to have a centralized resource [containing these capability statements]. That would make a big difference. That sounds somewhat simple; it sounds like it would be a work-in-progress; it sounds like each platform would have a product person occasionally update it; it would have to be an ongoing effort to be accurate and useful,” said Palmer. “The devil’s in the detail.”

Asked if FreeWheel already offers a form of capability statement, “I don’t think we have anything outside of our internal documentation that our customers have access to on our hub,” Strickland said.

OpenX did not provide information on whether the company offers a form of capability statement as of this writing.

Both FreeWheel’s Strickland and an OpenX spokesperson said their respective companies are committed to participating in that centralized resource containing the capability statements, though. Eventually.

“We are all actively, with OpenX as well, working to build a more efficient and competitive marketplace where our premium video content can be recognized and monetized appropriately, and we are willing to do whatever it takes to make that happen,” said Strickland.

Maybe that work will start soon. CIMM is looking to organize a voluntary pilot program for companies to adopt the proposed baseline signals set and implement capability statements. When will that pilot start, though? TBD.

“If we’re going to pay LinkedIn’s usual premium pricing, we’d want more clarity on how LinkedIn plans to accurately target their users on these devices.”

During a session at Digiday’s recent Creator Strategies virtual event, YSL Beauty’s Hadley Musinsky and Night’s Sophie Bressler took the deal model apart step by step, from the brand’s first brief to the final post, and show how each side handles talent selection, creative alignment, and negotiation. #7190. WTF is a unified media platform?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Editor’s note: These technologies are ill-defined, please share feedback to “editorial@digiday.com” to talk more.

Ad tech has never been short on acronyms. Its latest crop includes: unified advertising platforms (UAPs), unified media platforms (UMPs), and unified buying platforms (UBPs).

This kind of language has added a new layer of confusion to the space, and ad executives across adland have been saying, “WTF…?” Digiday is here to help by trying to bring some clarity to the matter.

A simple way to approach the question is to think of such terms — UAP, UBP and UMP — as overlapping labels for technology that promises to make advertising easier to buy, manage and optimize across an increasingly fragmented media landscape.

Of course, AI is central to the pitch. Advocates of such technologies maintain that instead of separately operating campaigns across Google, Meta, Amazon and the open web, advertisers could potentially use one system to determine where money goes, then execute those decisions through the relevant platforms.

Some providers propose bringing buying and selling infrastructure together. Others want to sit above existing systems and coordinate them. This is an important distinction to keep in mind, as connective software is not the same as controlling how an advertiser spends its next dollar.

At its simplest, a UMP coordinates planning, buying, optimization, and measurement across multiple media channels. Its remit could include programmatic display, CTV, retail media, search, social, and direct publisher relationships.

According to most sources, the idea is not necessarily to replace every existing media trading infrastructure, i.e., demand-side and supply-side platforms, etc., but to make them work together more efficiently.

That is distinct from the traditional remit of a DSP, many of which have spent years expanding the inventory and formats available through their own buying interfaces. A unified platform, by contrast, promises to operate across buying environments that no single DSP controls.

Jay Friedman, co-founder of CartographAI, told Digiday that the emerging proposition is about budget fluidity, not simply consolidating DSPs and SSPs. “We define a UMP as a platform that executes buys across all primary digital channels (search, social, programmatic, and direct). This may or may not include agents,” Friedman told Digiday. “Traditionally, this has been the domain of agency order management systems,” he said, noting how several new entrants are hoping to challenge these players.

The terms overlap, but they differ in what they promise to unify. The chart below traces that range, from handling an individual transaction to coordinating campaigns across channels and deciding where budgets go.

Keith Weisberg of Unplugged Collective told Digiday UBPs would initially come to market as a repackaging of existing tools. He said the deeper shift would come when buyers could bring their data to the places ads run and delegate spending decisions to AI agents.

“I’d treat that as a net-new product, not a DSP feature,” he said. “However, we’re all plugged into DSPs, and even if it’s a new product, it’ll roll out via our current commercial relationships, because that trust (and legal contracting) took years.”

Ana Milicevic, co-founder of Sparrow Advisers, described the labels as directional distinctions rather than settled product categories. She added, “It’s not all just a collection of black boxes. Then that’s a net new layer in the stack.” The key distinction is whether a platform merely pushes buttons in existing interfaces or makes independent decisions about where budgets go.

Ciarán O’Kane, general partner at First Party Capital, was more skeptical about novelty. In wording expressly cleared for attribution, he characterized UMPs and UBPs as “meta DSPs” that act as workflow layers on top of existing systems by plugging into APIs. He also said many emerging players in the space that use this terminology are revisiting approaches the industry has tried before.

Media-buying teams have long struggled with the fragmentation of conducting ad campaigns using different buying interfaces, datasets, and measurement methods. AI can make the operational work of combining and interpreting those inputs less expensive, but it does not automatically create comparable performance data or independent buying authority.

“The frustration is very, very old. What’s new now is that AI is letting us operationally skip through some hurdles that were previously insurmountable,” Milicevic told Digiday. Tasks that once demanded a substantial analytics stack and specialist labor can increasingly be assisted by repeatable prompts and automated processes.

The commercial question is whether cheaper orchestration creates an entirely new buyer or simply a more convenient way to operate existing platforms. For advertisers, that distinction determines whether the new technology saves money, reallocates money, or merely adds another vendor to pay.

In an Oct. 8 blog post, titled “universal buying platform,” Apostrophe co-founders Brian O’Kelley and Anne Coghlan outlined a broader buying layer intended to connect intent, execution, and measurement across advertising environments, citing the recent launch of Amazon Ads Agent as an example of this trend.

Their argument is a public proposal, not evidence that a single independent platform already performs every part of the workflow at scale — O’Kelley did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment by press time.

Other companies are approaching the problem from different directions, with Olyzon — an entity that used the term “agentic orchestration platform” in its recent CTV launch material — serving as one example.

Jules Minvielle, co-founder and CEO of Olyzon, told Digiday the company does not own inventory and uses agents to coordinate buying through existing platforms, with advertiser-controlled approvals. “A DSP is one place where you can buy. Orchestration is all the work around the buy: building the plan, deciding where each dollar goes, activating across every platform, watching delivery, measuring, and feeding what worked into the next plan,” he wrote in an emailed statement, adding that uneven API access and inconsistent measurement remain substantial obstacles.

Ravi Patel, co-founder and CEO of SWYM.ai, drew a distinction between a common interface and control over the budget. “It can manage the ‘what’ and the ‘how much.’ It can’t manage the ‘which impression.’ That distinction is the whole story,” he explained, adding that campaign setup and pacing across platforms are already possible through APIs. However, impression-level optimization within the major walled gardens remains in their own systems.

Lucas Cridland, CEO of Basin Media, questioned whether agents are changing the underlying buying process. “At the moment, many environments are agentically operating in the same supply chain flow. They’re just doing it with agents buying; they’re not radically changing the way the process works. And that’s where the real edge exists.”

Not necessarily. Many of the new propositions depend on DSPs continuing to execute transactions, access inventory, and optimize within their own environments. The change may be who decides which platform gets the advertiser’s budget in the first place.

Milicevic maintained that “value always accrues to whoever is closest to the decision or outright owns the decision.”

Ergo, if DSPs cannot move upstream into genuine cross-channel decision-making, they risk becoming “dumb pipes,” i.e., a commoditized, essential infrastructure whose underlying value is less visible to buyers.

Incumbent DSPs, however, have established advertiser relationships, bidding technology, data integrations and scale. They could develop their own orchestration capabilities. A new intermediary would need to show that its independence produces better decisions, rather than simply adding another fee, according to several sources consulted for this piece.

Agencies already coordinate campaigns across channels, negotiate buying arrangements and manage the systems needed to execute them. Friedman, a former long-time agency-lead, argued that unified platforms could challenge agency order-management systems while bringing neglected channels, including creator-led media, into a common workflow.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

The biggest obstacle may be the very platforms that independent systems are supposed to coordinate. Google, Meta, and Amazon control their own buying tools, data, and optimization systems. Access through an API does not necessarily provide the granular information or authority required to compare results and independently move budgets.

Trust is also an issue. A unified interface can display reports from multiple platforms without proving that the outcomes are comparable. Attribution windows, campaign objectives, and self-reported measurement differ across channels, meaning an independent buying layer needs a credible basis for deciding that one dollar is better spent in one environment than another.

Friedman was skeptical that interoperability would resolve the underlying problems, and highlighted how Google and Meta are unlikely to make their systems interoperable. He further questioned whether agencies could build the context and routing infrastructure needed to translate employees’ instructions into accurate buying decisions, even if every platform supported the Model Context Protocol. “Interoperability won’t solve anything,” he told Digiday.

Greg MacDonald, founder of Chelsea Strategies, said addressing media buying’s growing complexity could reduce spending on its mechanics and, over time, potentially improve advertiser outcomes. However, he remained cautious about how far unified platforms could go. “I’m skeptical that the walled gardens will give buyers more control than they have today,” he told Digiday, pointing to persistent difficulties with frequency capping across programmatic formats, let alone disconnected marketplaces.

“But you don’t have to solve every problem, and certainly not on day one to be useful,” MacDonald added. He compared the opportunity with programmatic guaranteed buying, which made the insertion-order process more efficient without fundamentally changing direct media buying.

Meanwhile, Milicevic said the more plausible near-term customer may be a multichannel advertiser between the largest global brands and small businesses that largely rely on a single platform. She also stressed the need for independent validation of results, rather than taking each platform’s own reporting at face value.

AI agents could help automate campaign setup, inventory discovery, negotiation, execution, and reporting. That makes agentic advertising relevant to the unified-platform debate, but not synonymous with it: automating tasks is different from making accountable, cross-channel budget decisions.

Unplugged Collective maps seven ways AI could change advertising (see graphic below). Per Weisberg, two are central to the unified buying debate: how agents decide where to spend and how they carry out those decisions across platforms.

Industry initiatives such as AdCP and IAB Tech Lab’s agentic work aim to establish ways for systems to communicate and execute workflows. Technical standards could reduce integration costs, but they cannot by themselves force walled gardens to disclose comparable data or surrender commercial control.

Seasoned industry observers have seen enough buzz phrases come and go to feel confident enough to make tongue-in-cheek observations when a new acronym becomes de rigueur among the industry’s chattering classes.

“I saw ‘UMP’ and I thought: ad network,” quipped Terence Kawaja, CEO, LUMA Partners, a prominent investment bank in the space, speaking on stage at this year’s Prebid Summit – channeling the industry adage, “everything is an ad network.”

Meanwhile, one prominent industry source, who requested anonymity to preserve industry relationships, dismissed the latest acronyms as another serving of “word salad.” However, the barbs, playful or otherwise, raise a palpable industry debate: As AI makes it cheaper to run campaigns across platforms, who gets to decide where the money goes?

The clearest test is whether advertisers entrust meaningful cross-channel allocation to an independent system — and can demonstrate that the decisions improve outcomes. Smaller-scale automation and campaign management do not, on their own, prove that a new market category has emerged.

Milicevic identified three indicators: real advertiser budgets moving under independent allocation, credible measurement outside any single walled garden, and changes in how marketing organizations structure teams. “If it’s truly structural, it’ll show up in the PNL, and if it’s slideware and fluff, then that’ll make its way into award entries and kind of the PR circuit,” she said.

For now, the most useful distinction is between controlling the infrastructure through which ads are purchased and controlling the decision about where the money goes. The latter is the more ambitious promise. Whether anyone can deliver it independently, at scale, and at a cost advertisers will accept remains unresolved.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

In Q3 earnings, The Trade Desk’s leadership reveals it is searching for answers.

Japanese holdco appointed Mike Nakamura as entertainment and sport chief, as media revenues slip in most markets.
